this interpretation is confirmed by sub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nPermutation Granger (PermG) tests find 0/8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jump to 6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.\nThis transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary: $C = 1{-}(1{-}\\alpha)^{10}$ yields $99.75\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ versus $99.90\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, a difference of 0.15 percentage points, yet detection jumps from null to reliable.\nSigmoid regression on the 13-point $T{=}10$ grid yields $\\alpha_c = 0.483 \\pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$); dense $\\alpha$ sampling (17~values) constrains the onset steepness to $k{=}15.7$ (95\\% CI $[9.4, 28.2]$), a steep but finite-width sigmoid (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}).\nThis single-$T$ estimate is consistent with the cross-$T$ validated model (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}), which predicts how the detection boundary shifts with observation length ($\\gamma{=}2.08$).\nThe transition is not a literal step function ($\\Delta\\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$ favoring sigmoid; Appendix~\\ref{app:sigmoid_shape}), but its steepness (${\\approx}3{\\times}$ smooth-model predictions; \\S\\ref{sec:theory}) exceeds what PermG power curves alone produce.\nAt $T{=}10$, PermG power is ${\\approx}37\\%$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}), partly amplifying the $0/8{\\to}6/8$ sharpness; however, onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$, ${\\approx}3{\\times}$ smooth predictions) and cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\\to}6/8$ at $\\alpha{=}0.48{\\to}0.50$) argue for a genuine, power-modulated transition.\nA Fisher exact test corroborates the categorical jump: 0/64 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha \\leq 0.45$ versus 18/32 at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ ($p = 3.45 \\times 10^{-11}$); sub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) are consistent with proximity to the power boundary (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nA systematic robustness analysis (1{,}160 configurations) confirms specification-invariance of the onset.\n\n\\paragraph{Canary scoping.}\nFirst-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.\nCanary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \\to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, 6 pairs per seed), while canary$\\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \\approx 0$, $p = 0.99$): the apparent perplexity association is a shared-trend artifact.\nAll canary claims are therefore scoped to diversity metrics only.\nThis scoping reflects a genuine temporal structure difference, not selective power loss from differencing: $|r|$ retention is ${\\geq}95\\%$ for both channels, but canary$\\to$diversity correlations peak at lagged offsets (81\\% at lag${<}0$, median${=}-1$) while canary$\\to$perplexity correlations are contemporaneous (50\\% at lag${=}0$), producing $20$--$28{\\times}$ lower Granger $F$ for perplexity (Appendix~\\ref{app:channel_effect_size}; perplexity $n{=}8$, limited sample).\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed $\\times$ 4~seeds; mauve excluded) reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; directional asymmetry is robust across all WikiText conditions.\nIndependent of the Granger test, the degradation cascade confirms temporal precedence: entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ onset at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Kendall $\\tau{=}0.337$; \\S\\ref{sec:cascade}, Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}), with supporting change-point analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:change_point}).\nThe canary mechanism also exhibits a blind spot: at $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null ($-5.3\\%$ versus $-4.6{\\pm}0.4\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$).\nThe gen-1 entropy deviation spans three orders of magnitude across the $\\alpha$ range: $0.5\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ to $60\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (relative to null variability), explaining why the blind zone persists despite increasing contamination.\\footnote{Seed~43 at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ shows 3/8 versus 0/8 for seed~42; no other sub-threshold level exhibits seed-dependent detection.}\nBelow $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal in the canary metrics, though Gaussian MI remains positive (Appendix~\\ref{app:mi_analysis}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\textwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead across all four seeds (shaded: inter-seed range). This pattern corresponds to 24/24 forward and 0/24 reverse FDR-significant canary$\\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed, mauve excluded).}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{PermG dose-response profile (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). Sigmoid fit (shaded 95\\% CI) yields $\\alpha_c{=}0.483$. Four regimes: blind ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.45$, 0/8), onset ($\\alpha{\\approx}0.50$, 6/8), sync-dip ($\\alpha{=}0.60$, 0/8), and detection ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$).}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}; full per-method table in Appendix~\\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals four detection regimes: \\emph{blind} ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, 0/8 FDR-significant pairs), \\emph{onset} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.50$, 6/8), \\emph{non-monotonic dip} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.60$, 0/8), and \\emph{detection} ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, 6/8 across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.55$, PermG returns 0/8 FDR-significant pairs despite marginal $p$-values ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}}{=}0.062$--$0.092$); differenced cross-correlation detects 8/8 bidirectionally at this level, confirming that a lead-lag signal exists but falls below PermG's directional detection threshold.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.60$, PermG returns $0/8$ at $T{\\leq}20$ but recovers to $5/8$ at $T{=}25$, confirming a transient dip that $\\sqrt{T}$ power eventually overcomes (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_comparison}).\n\nMethod choice is itself a major source of variability: at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, direction-checked forward significance spans 0\\% to 75\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), with $\\kappa{=}0.185$; a specification curve (18~variants; Appendix~\\ref{app:spec_curve}) confirms onset invariance.\nTask degradation corroborates: perplexity jumps $+31\\%$ between $\\alpha{=}0.50$ and $0.52$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:task_degradation}).\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Forward-direction FDR significance rates (\\%) across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, fp32; $3\\sigma$ threshold calibration, lag-direction filtering for cross-correlation methods). Each cell: fraction of 8 validated pairs at BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$). PermG achieves the lowest null-control FPR among directional methods (seed-42: 0/8; pooled 6-seed: 11.1\\%) with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & $\\alpha{=}0.0$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ & $\\alpha{=}1.0$ & FPR$_\\text{null}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr          & 75   & 62.5 & 62.5 & 0    & 0    & 75  \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr         & 0    & 25   & 12.5 & 0    & 0    & 0   \\\\\nThreshold ($3\\sigma$) & 37.5 & 62.5 & 100  & 100  & 25   & 37.5 \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger       & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{0}   \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto$^\\S$  & 12.5 & 37.5 & 50   & 100  & 12.5 & 12.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\S$TY directional assignment under verification; row retained for completeness.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Pooled FPR (3 seeds, cluster-bootstrap 95\\% CI): \\S\\ref{sec:mmct}.} \\\\\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\nThe detection boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ decreases monotonically: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with $T^{-2.08}$ power scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column traces the sharpest validation: $0/8$ at $T{\\leq}10$, $6/8$ at $T{=}15$ (dual-seed), $5/8$ at $T{=}20$, $6/8$ at $T{=}25$.\nThe systematic 2/8 gap reflects MAUVE floor saturation ($\\mu{=}0.029$, SNR $4.68$ vs diversity $6.40$--$8.20$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nCross-seed replication shows PermG within ${\\pm}1$, though boundary cells exhibit sensitivity: $[0, 6]/8$ at ($T{=}10$, $\\alpha{=}0.50$); $[0, 3]/8$ at ($T{=}20$, $\\alpha{=}0.60$).\nMinimum $T$ for 80\\% power at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ is approximately $T{=}30$ (extrapolated from simulation; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.60$ dip dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), validating $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling; predict-then-verify (commit \\texttt{bad72cc}) yields 2/4 boundary predictions within CI (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_prediction_timeline}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe evaluate PermG across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families, scales 345M--6.9B, and two domains (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}).\nPermG reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions where entropy declines monotonically before diversity; C4 yields strongest detection (Pythia-1.4B: 2/8 WikiText $\\to$ 8/8 C4).\nNon-confirming conditions span precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8, entropy 0/4).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture PermG forward detection ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, 15 model-seed combinations across five families and two domains).}\n\\label{tab:generalization}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrcl}\n\\toprule\nModel & Params & Domain & PermG fwd \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-410M    & 410M & Wiki & \\textbf{6/8} \\\\\nPythia-1.4B    & 1.4B & Wiki & 2--6/8$^\\dagger$ \\\\\nPythia-6.9B    & 6.9B & Wiki & \\textbf{7/8}$^*$ \\\\\nGPT-2-medium   & 345M & Wiki & \\textbf{6/8}$^\\S$ \\\\\nOPT-1.3B       & 1.3B & Wiki & \\textbf{6/8} \\\\\nQwen3.5-0.8B   & 0.8B & Wiki & \\textbf{8/8} \\\\\nSmolLM3-3B     & 3.0B & Wiki & 3/8$^\\ddagger$ \\\\\nPadding-410M   & 410M & Wiki & \\textbf{6/8} \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nPythia-1B      & 1.0B & C4   & \\textbf{8/8} \\\\\nPythia-1.4B    & 1.4B & C4   & \\textbf{8/8} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Seed-dep.\\ (s42: 2/8, s43: 6/8); 8-pair incl.\\ MAUVE} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\footnotesize \\phantom{$^\\dagger$}(floor ${\\sim}0.033$ at 1.4B; 6-pair in Table~\\ref{tab:14b_phase}). $^*$bf16. $^\\ddagger$ECE-only (ent.\\ 0/4).} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\S$s42 fp32: 6/8 fwd, 0 rev; s43: 6/8 both; bf16: 0/8.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\footnotesize 2 seeds each for Pythia-410M/1.4B, GPT-2, OPT, Pythia-1B C4.} \\\\\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Canary-Based Intervention (Proof of Concept)}\n\\label{sec:intervention}\n\nAs an illustrative proof-of-concept under favorable conditions ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, full replacement), we implement predictive cessation: training switches to pure real data when token entropy drops ${>}15\\%$ from generation~0.\nThe 15\\% threshold was selected post-hoc (robust band $[10\\%, 22\\%]$, zero false alarms; Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention_sensitivity}).\nThe predictive trigger fires at generation~1 (one generation before a reactive distinct-1 baseline), reducing peak perplexity by 64\\% ($56.99$ vs.\\ $157.09$; Figure~\\ref{fig:intervention}) in both Pythia-410M and 1.4B.\nThis single-setting ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) demonstration is not rigorously evaluated (CUSUM comparison: Appendix~\\ref{app:csmp}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.6\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_intervention.pdf}\n\\caption{Canary-triggered intervention (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$). Predictive cessation on entropy (generation~1) reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% versus reactive triggering on distinct-1 (generation~2).}\n\\label{fig:intervention}\n\\end{figure}\n",

this interpretation is confirmed by sub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nPermutation Granger (PermG) tests find 0/8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jump to 6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.\nThis transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary: $C = 1{-}(1{-}\\alpha)^{10}$ yields $99.75\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ versus $99.90\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, a difference of 0.15 percentage points, yet detection jumps from null to reliable.\nSigmoid regression on the 13-point $T{=}10$ grid yields $\\alpha_c = 0.483 \\pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$); dense $\\alpha$ sampling (17~values) constrains the onset steepness to $k{=}15.7$ (95\\% CI $[9.4, 28.2]$), a steep but finite-width sigmoid (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}).\nThis single-$T$ estimate is consistent with the cross-$T$ validated model (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}), which predicts how the detection boundary shifts with observation length ($\\gamma{=}2.08$).\nThe transition is not a literal step function ($\\Delta\\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$ favoring sigmoid; Appendix~\\ref{app:sigmoid_shape}), but its steepness (${\\approx}3{\\times}$ smooth-model predictions; \\S\\ref{sec:theory}) exceeds what PermG power curves alone produce.\nAt $T{=}10$, PermG power is ${\\approx}37\\%$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}), partly amplifying the $0/8{\\to}6/8$ sharpness; however, onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$, ${\\approx}3{\\times}$ smooth predictions) and cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\\to}6/8$ at $\\alpha{=}0.48{\\to}0.50$) argue for a genuine, power-modulated transition.\nA Fisher exact test corroborates the categorical jump: 0/64 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha \\leq 0.45$ versus 18/32 at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ ($p = 3.45 \\times 10^{-11}$); sub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) are consistent with proximity to the power boundary (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nA systematic robustness analysis (1{,}160 configurations) confirms specification-invariance of the onset.",
    "newString": "\\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nDiversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\\%$ even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (3-seed mean).\nDespite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger detectability is sharply regime-dependent.\nPermG finds 0/8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.\n\nThis transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary: $C = 1{-}(1{-}\\alpha)^{10}$ yields $99.75\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ versus $99.90\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, a difference of 0.15 percentage points, yet detection jumps from null to reliable.\nThe onset reflects PermG's statistical power crossing the detection threshold, not the emergence of a new physical signal; sub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) confirm proximity to the boundary (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nSigmoid regression on the 13-point $T{=}10$ grid yields $\\alpha_c = 0.483 \\pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}); dense $\\alpha$ sampling (17~values) constrains onset steepness to $k{=}15.7$ (95\\% CI $[9.4, 28.2]$), a steep but finite-width sigmoid ($\\Delta\\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$ vs.\\ step function; Appendix~\\ref{app:sigmoid_shape}).\nThis steepness (${\\approx}3{\\times}$ smooth-model predictions; \\S\\ref{sec:theory}) and cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\\to}6/8$) argue for a genuine, power-modulated transition rather than a statistical artifact.\nA Fisher exact test corroborates: 0/64 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha \\leq 0.45$ versus 18/32 at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ ($p = 3.45 \\times 10^{-11}$); robustness analysis across 1{,}160 configurations confirms specification-invariance.",

{

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Canary scoping.}\nFirst-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.\nCanary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \\to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, 6 pairs per seed), while canary$\\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \\approx 0$, $p = 0.99$): the apparent perplexity association is a shared-trend artifact.\nAll canary claims are therefore scoped to diversity metrics only.\nThis scoping reflects a genuine temporal structure difference, not selective power loss from differencing: $|r|$ retention is ${\\geq}95\\%$ for both channels, but canary$\\to$diversity correlations peak at lagged offsets (81\\% at lag${<}0$, median${=}-1$) while canary$\\to$perplexity correlations are contemporaneous (50\\% at lag${=}0$), producing $20$--$28{\\times}$ lower Granger $F$ for perplexity (Appendix~\\ref{app:channel_effect_size}; perplexity $n{=}8$, limited sample).\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed $\\times$ 4~seeds; mauve excluded) reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; directional asymmetry is robust across all WikiText conditions.\nIndependent of the Granger test, the degradation cascade confirms temporal precedence: entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ onset at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Kendall $\\tau{=}0.337$; \\S\\ref{sec:cascade}, Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}), with supporting change-point analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:change_point}).\nThe canary mechanism also exhibits a blind spot: at $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null ($-5.3\\%$ versus $-4.6{\\pm}0.4\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$).\nThe gen-1 entropy deviation spans three orders of magnitude across the $\\alpha$ range: $0.5\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ to $60\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (relative to null variability), explaining why the blind zone persists despite increasing contamination.\\footnote{Seed~43 at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ shows 3/8 versus 0/8 for seed~42; no other sub-threshold level exhibits seed-dependent detection.}\nBelow $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal in the canary metrics, though Gaussian MI remains positive (Appendix~\\ref{app:mi_analysis}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Canary scoping.}\nFirst-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.\nCanary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \\to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, 6 per seed), while canary$\\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \\approx 0$, $p = 0.99$): the apparent perplexity association is a shared-trend artifact~\\cite{granger1974spurious}.\nAll canary claims are therefore scoped to diversity metrics only.\nThis scoping reflects a genuine temporal structure difference: canary$\\to$diversity correlations peak at lagged offsets (81\\% at lag${<}0$, median${=}-1$), while canary$\\to$perplexity correlations are contemporaneous (50\\% at lag${=}0$), producing $20$--$28{\\times}$ lower Granger $F$ for perplexity (Appendix~\\ref{app:channel_effect_size}).\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; the degradation cascade independently confirms this ordering: entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions ($\\tau{=}0.337$; \\S\\ref{sec:cascade}, Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\n\n\\paragraph{Blind zone.}\nThe canary mechanism has a fundamental limitation: at $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null ($-5.3\\%$ versus $-4.6{\\pm}0.4\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$).\nThe gen-1 entropy deviation spans three orders of magnitude across the $\\alpha$ range ($0.5\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ to $60\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.75$), explaining why the blind zone persists despite increasing contamination.\\footnote{Seed~43 at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ shows 3/8 versus 0/8 for seed~42; no other sub-threshold level exhibits seed-dependent detection.}\nBelow $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal, though Gaussian MI remains positive (Appendix~\\ref{app:mi_analysis})."
}

          "-This scoping reflects a genuine temporal structure difference, not selective power loss from differencing: $|r|$ retention is ${\\geq}95\\%$ for both channels, but canary$\\to$diversity correlations peak at lagged offsets (81\\% at lag${<}0$, median${=}-1$) while canary$\\to$perplexity correlations are contemporaneous (50\\% at lag${=}0$), producing $20$--$28{\\times}$ lower Granger $F$ for perplexity (Appendix~\\ref{app:channel_effect_size}; perplexity $n{=}8$, limited sample).",
          "-At $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed $\\times$ 4~seeds; mauve excluded) reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; directional asymmetry is robust across all WikiText conditions.",
          "-Independent of the Granger test, the degradation cascade confirms temporal precedence: entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ onset at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Kendall $\\tau{=}0.337$; \\S\\ref{sec:cascade}, Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}), with supporting change-point analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:change_point}).",
          "-The canary mechanism also exhibits a blind spot: at $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null ($-5.3\\%$ versus $-4.6{\\pm}0.4\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$).",
          "-The gen-1 entropy deviation spans three orders of magnitude across the $\\alpha$ range: $0.5\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ to $60\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (relative to null variability), explaining why the blind zone persists despite increasing contamination.\\footnote{Seed~43 at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ shows 3/8 versus 0/8 for seed~42; no other sub-threshold level exhibits seed-dependent detection.}",
          "-Below $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal in the canary metrics, though Gaussian MI remains positive (Appendix~\\ref{app:mi_analysis}).",
          "+This scoping reflects a genuine temporal structure difference: canary$\\to$diversity correlations peak at lagged offsets (81\\% at lag${<}0$, median${=}-1$), while canary$\\to$perplexity correlations are contemporaneous (50\\% at lag${=}0$), producing $20$--$28{\\times}$ lower Granger $F$ for perplexity (Appendix~\\ref{app:channel_effect_size}).",
          "+At $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; the degradation cascade independently confirms this ordering: entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions ($\\tau{=}0.337$; \\S\\ref{sec:cascade}, Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).",
          " ",
          "+\\paragraph{Blind zone.}",
          "+The canary mechanism has a fundamental limitation: at $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null ($-5.3\\%$ versus $-4.6{\\pm}0.4\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$).",
          "+The gen-1 entropy deviation spans three orders of magnitude across the $\\alpha$ range ($0.5\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ to $60\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.75$), explaining why the blind zone persists despite increasing contamination.\\footnote{Seed~43 at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ shows 3/8 versus 0/8 for seed~42; no other sub-threshold level exhibits seed-dependent detection.}",
          "+Below $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal, though Gaussian MI remains positive (Appendix~\\ref{app:mi_analysis}).",

  "old_string": "We evaluate PermG across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families, scales 345M--6.9B, and two domains (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}).\nPermG reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions where entropy declines monotonically before diversity; C4 yields strongest detection (Pythia-1.4B: 2/8 WikiText $\\to$ 8/8 C4).\nNon-confirming conditions span precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8, entropy 0/4).",
  "new_string": "To test whether the phase-diagram structure is Pythia-specific, we evaluate PermG across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families, scales 345M--6.9B, and two domains (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}).\nForward detection reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions, confirming that the canary mechanism generalizes whenever entropy declines monotonically before diversity.\nDomain matters: C4 yields the strongest detection (Pythia-1.4B: 2/8 WikiText $\\to$ 8/8 C4), suggesting that domain-specific mode structure affects detectability.\nThe four non-confirming conditions each isolate a distinct failure mode: precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8, whose non-monotonic entropy violates Proposition~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads})."
}

  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}\nOur claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}; bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and our cluster-adjusted FPR CI ($[0\\%, 41\\%]$; \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}) means true Type~I error may be non-negligible.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-confirming cells.}\nFive $\\alpha{=}0.60$ cells are excluded from the boundary fit (sync-dip zone: 0/8 at $T{\\leq}20$, dissolving to 5/8 at $T{=}25$); the remaining 15/15 match theory (LOO-CV MAE${=}0.037$).\nThe systematic 2/8 gap reflects MAUVE floor saturation ($\\mu{=}0.029$).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-architecture failure modes.}\nFour of fifteen conditions do not confirm forward detection (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), spanning GPT-2 precision/confounding, Pythia-1.4B seed dependence, and SmolLM3-3B entropy decoupling (whose non-monotonic entropy violates Proposition~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}'s monotonic-decline assumption, correctly predicting failure).\nPhase-diagram structure is qualitatively preserved at 1.4B ($\\alpha_c$ onset consistent, sync-dip absent, forward detection seed-robust at $T{=}10$); directional asymmetry is seed-sensitive above onset (s43 reverse 6/6 at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.60$, $T{=}10$; s42: 0/6 across all conditions; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_14b}).\n\n\\paragraph{Implications.}\nThe ${\\geq}1$-generation entropy-diversity lag (14/15 experiments; Proposition~\\ref{prop:div_lag}) and PermG directional asymmetry (6/8 forward, 0/8 reverse) support a structural canary mechanism, though directionality is domain-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_discussion}).\nOur contribution is the phase-diagram methodology, not frontier-scale applicability ($>$7B untested); triggered cessation on entropy reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:intervention}).",
  "new_string": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of claims.}\nOur Granger-precedence claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}: bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and the cluster-adjusted FPR CI ($[1.2\\%, 30.4\\%]$; \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}) means true Type~I error may be non-negligible.\nFive $\\alpha{=}0.60$ cells are excluded from the boundary fit (sync-dip zone: 0/8 at $T{\\leq}20$, dissolving to 5/8 at $T{=}25$); the remaining 15/15 match theory (LOO-CV MAE${=}0.037$).\n\n\\paragraph{When the canary fails.}\nFour of fifteen cross-architecture conditions do not confirm forward detection (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), and each failure is interpretable.\nSmolLM3-3B's non-monotonic entropy violates Proposition~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}'s monotonic-decline assumption, correctly predicting failure; GPT-2's precision sensitivity and Pythia-1.4B's seed dependence expose practical deployment constraints.\nPhase-diagram structure is qualitatively preserved at 1.4B ($\\alpha_c$ onset consistent, sync-dip absent); directional asymmetry is seed-sensitive above onset (s43 reverse 6/6 at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.60$; s42: 0/6; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_14b}).\n\n\\paragraph{Broader implications.}\nThree findings carry practical weight.\nFirst, the ${\\geq}1$-generation entropy-diversity lag (14/15 experiments) provides an actionable early-warning window, and triggered cessation demonstrates that this window can be exploited (64\\% perplexity reduction; \\S\\ref{sec:intervention}).\nSecond, the method-sensitivity results (3\\% to 92\\% FPR on identical data) are a cautionary finding for any collapse monitoring effort: conclusions are only as reliable as the statistical method used to reach them.\nThird, the phase-diagram methodology itself transfers: the $\\alpha_c(T)$ framework provides a template for characterizing detection boundaries in other iterative degradation settings.\nOur contribution is this methodology, not frontier-scale applicability ($>$7B untested)."
}

    "oldString": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}\nOur claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}; bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and our cluster-adjusted FPR CI ($[0\\%, 41\\%]$; \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}) means true Type~I error may be non-negligible.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-confirming cells.}\nFive $\\alpha{=}0.60$ cells are excluded from the boundary fit (sync-dip zone: 0/8 at $T{\\leq}20$, dissolving to 5/8 at $T{=}25$); the remaining 15/15 match theory (LOO-CV MAE${=}0.037$).\nThe systematic 2/8 gap reflects MAUVE floor saturation ($\\mu{=}0.029$).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-architecture failure modes.}\nFour of fifteen conditions do not confirm forward detection (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), spanning GPT-2 precision/confounding, Pythia-1.4B seed dependence, and SmolLM3-3B entropy decoupling (whose non-monotonic entropy violates Proposition~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}'s monotonic-decline assumption, correctly predicting failure).\nPhase-diagram structure is qualitatively preserved at 1.4B ($\\alpha_c$ onset consistent, sync-dip absent, forward detection seed-robust at $T{=}10$); directional asymmetry is seed-sensitive above onset (s43 reverse 6/6 at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.60$, $T{=}10$; s42: 0/6 across all conditions; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_14b}).\n\n\\paragraph{Implications.}\nThe ${\\geq}1$-generation entropy-diversity lag (14/15 experiments; Proposition~\\ref{prop:div_lag}) and PermG directional asymmetry (6/8 forward, 0/8 reverse) support a structural canary mechanism, though directionality is domain-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_discussion}).\nOur contribution is the phase-diagram methodology, not frontier-scale applicability ($>$7B untested); triggered cessation on entropy reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:intervention}).",
    "newString": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of claims.}\nOur Granger-precedence claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}: bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and the cluster-adjusted FPR CI ($[1.2\\%, 30.4\\%]$; \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}) means true Type~I error may be non-negligible.\nFive $\\alpha{=}0.60$ cells are excluded from the boundary fit (sync-dip zone: 0/8 at $T{\\leq}20$, dissolving to 5/8 at $T{=}25$); the remaining 15/15 match theory (LOO-CV MAE${=}0.037$).\n\n\\paragraph{When the canary fails.}\nFour of fifteen cross-architecture conditions do not confirm forward detection (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), and each failure is interpretable.\nSmolLM3-3B's non-monotonic entropy violates Proposition~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}'s monotonic-decline assumption, correctly predicting failure; GPT-2's precision sensitivity and Pythia-1.4B's seed dependence expose practical deployment constraints.\nPhase-diagram structure is qualitatively preserved at 1.4B ($\\alpha_c$ onset consistent, sync-dip absent); directional asymmetry is seed-sensitive above onset (s43 reverse 6/6 at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.60$; s42: 0/6; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_14b}).\n\n\\paragraph{Broader implications.}\nThree findings carry practical weight.\nFirst, the ${\\geq}1$-generation entropy-diversity lag (14/15 experiments) provides an actionable early-warning window, and triggered cessation demonstrates that this window can be exploited (64\\% perplexity reduction; \\S\\ref{sec:intervention}).\nSecond, the method-sensitivity results (3\\% to 92\\% FPR on identical data) are a cautionary finding for any collapse monitoring effort: conclusions are only as reliable as the statistical method used to reach them.\nThird, the phase-diagram methodology itself transfers: the $\\alpha_c(T)$ framework provides a template for characterizing detection boundaries in other iterative degradation settings.\nOur contribution is this methodology, not frontier-scale applicability ($>$7B untested).",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "Our operationalization---declining detection rate of Granger-precedence canary$\\to$diversity links across generations---aligns most closely with Schaeffer et al.'s \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6 in their taxonomy), measuring how the generated distribution departs from the real-data baseline rather than tracking population risk or scaling-law exponents.",
    "newString": "Our operationalization (declining detection rate of Granger-precedence canary$\\to$diversity links across generations) aligns most closely with Schaeffer et al.'s \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6 in their taxonomy), measuring how the generated distribution departs from the real-data baseline rather than tracking population risk or scaling-law exponents.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Model collapse and canary metrics.}\nIterative training collapse was formalized by \\citet{shumailov2024curse} and \\citet{alemohammad2024mad}, who showed that recursively replacing real data with model-generated text causes progressive tail erosion; \\citet{dey2024breaking} demonstrated that data accumulation (rather than replacement) can mitigate collapse; \\citet{schaeffer2025position} systematize eight distinct notions of ``collapse'' that this literature conflates.\nOur operationalization---declining detection rate of Granger-precedence canary$\\to$diversity links across generations---aligns most closely with Schaeffer et al.'s \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6 in their taxonomy), measuring how the generated distribution departs from the real-data baseline rather than tracking population risk or scaling-law exponents.\nTheoretical analyses have sharpened these results: \\citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as a change in scaling-law exponents, \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved that collapse can occur even at vanishingly small synthetic fractions, \\citet{seddik2025statistical} provide a statistical analysis of iterative contamination dynamics, and \\citet{marchi2025challenging} challenge the universality of collapse under diverse training conditions.\nSeveral works have proposed monitoring metrics: \\citet{du2025scales} report ECE increases at 30\\% contamination before accuracy drops, \\citet{gambetta2024surplexity} introduces surplexity as a collapse detector, \\citet{drayson2025detection} show that machine-generated text detection can prevent collapse, and \\citet{li2025verification} propose verification-based mitigation.\n\\citet{shi2025closer} independently validate entropy as a collapse diagnostic in diffusion models ($r{=}0.91$ with generalization score), using it for diversity-aware sample selection.\n\\citet{du2026geometric} address a related but distinct phenomenon---mode collapse during generation---via geometric regulation of the representation trajectory; our work instead studies \\emph{iterative training} collapse across generations.\nWe differ from prior monitoring work in four respects (comparison table: Appendix~\\ref{app:method_comparison}): (i)~we compare an entire family of canary metrics; (ii)~we cross-validate across five statistical methods; (iii)~we map detectability as a function of contamination regime; and (iv)~we treat method-selection sensitivity itself as a primary finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Data mixing phase transitions.}\n\\citet{kazdan2025collapse} map the conditions under which synthetic data triggers collapse versus improves performance, \\citet{gu2024cmr} establish a scaling law for critical mixture ratios in continual pre-training, and \\citet{gu2025datamixing} show that data mixing can induce phase transitions in knowledge acquisition---a direct theoretical analogue to our detection threshold, where a critical mixing ratio separates qualitatively different detection regimes.\n\n\\paragraph{Critical transitions and lead-lag methodology.}\n\\citet{scheffer2009early} established that systems approaching tipping points exhibit generic early-warning signals (rising autocorrelation and variance); \\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a practical toolkit for estimating such signals, while \\citet{boettiger2012limits} quantified detection limits in short time series---directly relevant to iterative training where $T{=}5$--$20$.\nCrucially, \\citet{truong2026phase} prove that entropy collapse in iterative training is a first-order (discontinuous) phase transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down (CSD) early-warning signals.\nOur Granger-precedence approach circumvents this limitation by detecting temporal predictive improvement rather than variance/autocorrelation signatures, achieving ${\\geq}5/8$ forward detection in 12 of 15 above-boundary cells (three sync-dip exceptions at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ dissolve by $T{=}25$; \\S\\ref{sec:t20}).\nThe standard framework for testing temporal precedence is Granger causality~\\cite{granger1969investigating}, with neural extensions for nonlinear settings~\\citep{tank2021neural,marcinkevics2022neural,zeng2025kangci}; however, non-stationary series inflate rejection rates~\\cite{granger1974spurious}, and the Toda--Yamamoto procedure~\\cite{toda1995statistical} addresses this at reduced degrees of freedom severe in short series.\nWe use classical rather than neural Granger methods because $T{\\leq}25$ provides insufficient observations for neural network parameter estimation.\nThese methods are largely absent from the model collapse literature.\nOur analysis studies iterative from-scratch retraining; online or continual learning settings, where the model updates incrementally on streaming data, may exhibit different detection dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Analytical robustness.}\n\\citet{bell2022modeling} introduced multiverse analysis to ML, \\citet{coretta2023multidimensional} demonstrated that 46 teams reached different conclusions on identical data, and \\citet{kapoor2024reforms} synthesized these concerns into a consensus checklist.\nOur work applies this perspective to model collapse: five methods across multiple contamination regimes produce conclusions ranging from universal significance to complete null.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

          "-Our operationalization---declining detection rate of Granger-precedence canary$\\to$diversity links across generations---aligns most closely with Schaeffer et al.'s \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6 in their taxonomy), measuring how the generated distribution departs from the real-data baseline rather than tracking population risk or scaling-law exponents.",
          "+Our operationalization (declining detection rate of Granger-precedence canary$\\to$diversity links across generations) aligns most closely with Schaeffer et al.'s \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6 in their taxonomy), measuring how the generated distribution departs from the real-data baseline rather than tracking population risk or scaling-law exponents.",

\\citet{dey2024breaking} demonstrated that data accumulation (rather than replacement) can mitigate collapse; \\citet{schaeffer2025position} systematize eight distinct notions of ``collapse'' that this literature conflates.\nOur operationalization (declining detection rate of Granger-precedence canary$\\to$diversity links across generations) aligns most closely with Schaeffer et al.'s \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6 in their taxonomy), measuring how the generated distribution departs from the real-data baseline rather than tracking population risk or scaling-law exponents.\nTheoretical analyses have sharpened these results: \\citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as a change in scaling-law exponents, \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved that collapse can occur even at vanishingly small synthetic fractions, \\citet{seddik2025statistical} provide a statistical analysis of iterative contamination dynamics, and \\citet{marchi2025challenging} challenge the universality of collapse under diverse training conditions.\nSeveral works have proposed monitoring metrics: \\citet{du2025scales} report ECE increases at 30\\% contamination before accuracy drops, \\citet{gambetta2024surplexity} introduces surplexity as a collapse detector, \\citet{drayson2025detection} show that machine-generated text detection can prevent collapse, and \\citet{li2025verification} propose verification-based mitigation.\n\\citet{shi2025closer} independently validate entropy as a collapse diagnostic in diffusion models ($r{=}0.91$ with generalization score), using it for diversity-aware sample selection.\n\\citet{du2026geometric} address a related but distinct phenomenon---mode collapse during generation---via geometric regulation of the representation trajectory; our work instead studies \\emph{iterative training} collapse across generations.\nWe differ from prior monitoring work in four respects (comparison table: Appendix~\\ref{app:method_comparison}): (i)~we compare an entire family of canary metrics; (ii)~we cross-validate across five statistical methods; (iii)~we map detectability as a function of contamination regime; and (iv)~we treat method-selection sensitivity itself as a primary finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Data mixing phase transitions.}\n\\citet{kazdan2025collapse} map the conditions under which synthetic data triggers collapse versus improves performance, \\citet{gu2024cmr} establish a scaling law for critical mixture ratios in continual pre-training, and \\citet{gu2025datamixing} show that data mixing can induce phase transitions in knowledge acquisition---a direct theoretical analogue to our detection threshold, where a critical mixing ratio separates qualitatively different detection regimes.\n\n\\paragraph{Critical transitions and lead-lag methodology.}\n\\citet{scheffer2009early} established that systems approaching tipping points exhibit generic early-warning signals (rising autocorrelation and variance); \\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a practical toolkit for estimating such signals, while \\citet{boettiger2012limits} quantified detection limits in short time series---directly relevant to iterative training where $T{=}5$--$20$.\nCrucially, \\citet{truong2026phase} prove that entropy collapse in iterative training is a first-order (discontinuous) phase transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down (CSD) early-warning signals.\nOur Granger-precedence approach circumvents this limitation by detecting temporal predictive improvement rather than variance/autocorrelation signatures, achieving ${\\geq}5/8$ forward detection in 12 of 15 above-boundary cells (three sync-dip exceptions at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ dissolve by $T{=}25$; \\S\\ref{sec:t20}).\nThe standard framework for testing temporal precedence is Granger causality~\\cite{granger1969investigating}, with neural extensions for nonlinear settings~\\citep{tank2021neural,marcinkevics2022neural,zeng2025kangci}; however, non-stationary series inflate rejection rates~\\cite{granger1974spurious}, and the Toda--Yamamoto procedure~\\cite{toda1995statistical} addresses this at reduced degrees of freedom severe in short series.\nWe use classical rather than neural Granger methods because $T{\\leq}25$ provides insufficient observations for neural network parameter estimation.\nThese methods are largely absent from the model collapse literature.\nOur analysis studies iterative from-scratch retraining; online or continual learning settings, where the model updates incrementally on streaming data, may exhibit different detection dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Analytical robustness.}\n\\citet{bell2022modeling} introduced multiverse analysis to ML, \\citet{coretta2023multidimensional} demonstrated that 46 teams reached different conclusions on identical data, and \\citet{kapoor2024reforms} synthesized these concerns into a consensus checklist.\nOur work applies this perspective to model collapse: five methods across multiple contamination regimes produce conclusions ranging from universal significance to complete null.\n",

          " \\citet{shi2025closer} independently validate entropy as a collapse diagnostic in diffusion models ($r{=}0.91$ with generalization score), using it for diversity-aware sample selection.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Model collapse and canary metrics.}\nIterative training collapse was formalized by \\citet{shumailov2024curse} and \\citet{alemohammad2024mad}, who showed that recursively replacing real data with model-generated text causes progressive tail erosion; \\citet{dey2024breaking} demonstrated that data accumulation (rather than replacement) can mitigate collapse; \\citet{schaeffer2025position} systematize eight distinct notions of ``collapse'' that this literature conflates.\nOur operationalization (declining detection rate of Granger-precedence canary$\\to$diversity links across generations) aligns most closely with Schaeffer et al.'s \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6 in their taxonomy), measuring how the generated distribution departs from the real-data baseline rather than tracking population risk or scaling-law exponents.\nTheoretical analyses have sharpened these results: \\citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as a change in scaling-law exponents, \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved that collapse can occur even at vanishingly small synthetic fractions, \\citet{seddik2025statistical} provide a statistical analysis of iterative contamination dynamics, and \\citet{marchi2025challenging} challenge the universality of collapse under diverse training conditions.\nSeveral works have proposed monitoring metrics: \\citet{du2025scales} report ECE increases at 30\\% contamination before accuracy drops, \\citet{gambetta2024surplexity} introduces surplexity as a collapse detector, \\citet{drayson2025detection} show that machine-generated text detection can prevent collapse, and \\citet{li2025verification} propose verification-based mitigation.\n\\citet{shi2025closer} independently validate entropy as a collapse diagnostic in diffusion models ($r{=}0.91$ with generalization score), using it for diversity-aware sample selection.\n\\citet{du2026geometric} address a related but distinct phenomenon (mode collapse during generation) via geometric regulation of the representation trajectory; our work instead studies \\emph{iterative training} collapse across generations.\nWe differ from prior monitoring work in four respects (comparison table: Appendix~\\ref{app:method_comparison}): (i)~we compare an entire family of canary metrics; (ii)~we cross-validate across five statistical methods; (iii)~we map detectability as a function of contamination regime; and (iv)~we treat method-selection sensitivity itself as a primary finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Data mixing phase transitions.}\nThe observation that critical mixture ratios separate qualitatively different outcomes has precedent: \\citet{kazdan2025collapse} map conditions under which synthetic data triggers collapse versus improves performance, and \\citet{gu2024cmr} establish a scaling law for critical mixture ratios in continual pre-training.\n\\citet{gu2025datamixing} show that data mixing can induce phase transitions in knowledge acquisition, a direct theoretical analogue to our detection boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Critical transitions and lead-lag methodology.}\n\\citet{scheffer2009early} established that systems approaching tipping points exhibit generic early-warning signals (rising autocorrelation and variance); \\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a practical toolkit for estimating such signals, while \\citet{boettiger2012limits} quantified detection limits in short time series---directly relevant to iterative training where $T{=}5$--$20$.\nCrucially, \\citet{truong2026phase} prove that entropy collapse in iterative training is a first-order (discontinuous) phase transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down (CSD) early-warning signals.\nOur Granger-precedence approach circumvents this limitation by detecting temporal predictive improvement rather than variance/autocorrelation signatures, achieving ${\\geq}5/8$ forward detection in 12 of 15 above-boundary cells (three sync-dip exceptions at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ dissolve by $T{=}25$; \\S\\ref{sec:t20}).\nThe standard framework for testing temporal precedence is Granger causality~\\cite{granger1969investigating}, with neural extensions for nonlinear settings~\\citep{tank2021neural,marcinkevics2022neural,zeng2025kangci}; however, non-stationary series inflate rejection rates~\\cite{granger1974spurious}, and the Toda--Yamamoto procedure~\\cite{toda1995statistical} addresses this at reduced degrees of freedom severe in short series.\nWe use classical rather than neural Granger methods because $T{\\leq}25$ provides insufficient observations for neural network parameter estimation.\nThese methods are largely absent from the model collapse literature.\nOur analysis studies iterative from-scratch retraining; online or continual learning settings, where the model updates incrementally on streaming data, may exhibit different detection dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Analytical robustness.}\n\\citet{bell2022modeling} introduced multiverse analysis to ML, \\citet{coretta2023multidimensional} demonstrated that 46 teams reached different conclusions on identical data, and \\citet{kapoor2024reforms} synthesized these concerns into a consensus checklist.\nOur work applies this perspective to model collapse: five methods across multiple contamination regimes produce conclusions ranging from universal significance to complete null.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 16,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 16,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [

          "-Crucially, \\citet{truong2026phase} prove that entropy collapse in iterative training is a first-order (discontinuous) phase transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down (CSD) early-warning signals.",
          "+\\citet{truong2026phase} prove that entropy collapse in iterative training is a first-order (discontinuous) phase transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down (CSD) early-warning signals.",
          " Our Granger-precedence approach circumvents this limitation by detecting temporal predictive improvement rather than variance/autocorrelation signatures, achieving ${\\geq}5/8$ forward detection in 12 of 15 above-boundary cells (three sync-dip exceptions at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ dissolve by $T{=}25$; \\S\\ref{sec:t20}).",
          " The standard framework for testing temporal precedence is Granger causality~\\cite{granger1969investigating}, with neural extensions for nonlinear settings~\\citep{tank2021neural,marcinkevics2022neural,zeng2025kangci}; however, non-stationary series inflate rejection rates~\\cite{granger1974spurious}, and the Toda--Yamamoto procedure~\\cite{toda1995statistical} addresses this at reduced degrees of freedom severe in short series.",

    "newString": "\\citet{boettiger2012limits} quantified detection limits in short time series, directly relevant to iterative training where $T{=}5$--$20$.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Model collapse and canary metrics.}\nIterative training collapse was formalized by \\citet{shumailov2024curse} and \\citet{alemohammad2024mad}, who showed that recursively replacing real data with model-generated text causes progressive tail erosion; \\citet{dey2024breaking} demonstrated that data accumulation (rather than replacement) can mitigate collapse; \\citet{schaeffer2025position} systematize eight distinct notions of ``collapse'' that this literature conflates.\nOur operationalization (declining detection rate of Granger-precedence canary$\\to$diversity links across generations) aligns most closely with Schaeffer et al.'s \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6 in their taxonomy), measuring how the generated distribution departs from the real-data baseline rather than tracking population risk or scaling-law exponents.\nTheoretical analyses have sharpened these results: \\citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as a change in scaling-law exponents, \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved that collapse can occur even at vanishingly small synthetic fractions, \\citet{seddik2025statistical} provide a statistical analysis of iterative contamination dynamics, and \\citet{marchi2025challenging} challenge the universality of collapse under diverse training conditions.\nSeveral works have proposed monitoring metrics: \\citet{du2025scales} report ECE increases at 30\\% contamination before accuracy drops, \\citet{gambetta2024surplexity} introduces surplexity as a collapse detector, \\citet{drayson2025detection} show that machine-generated text detection can prevent collapse, and \\citet{li2025verification} propose verification-based mitigation.\n\\citet{shi2025closer} independently validate entropy as a collapse diagnostic in diffusion models ($r{=}0.91$ with generalization score), using it for diversity-aware sample selection.\n\\citet{du2026geometric} address a related but distinct phenomenon (mode collapse during generation) via geometric regulation of the representation trajectory; our work instead studies \\emph{iterative training} collapse across generations.\nWe differ from prior monitoring work in four respects (comparison table: Appendix~\\ref{app:method_comparison}): (i)~we compare an entire family of canary metrics; (ii)~we cross-validate across five statistical methods; (iii)~we map detectability as a function of contamination regime; and (iv)~we treat method-selection sensitivity itself as a primary finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Data mixing phase transitions.}\nThe observation that critical mixture ratios separate qualitatively different outcomes has precedent: \\citet{kazdan2025collapse} map conditions under which synthetic data triggers collapse versus improves performance, and \\citet{gu2024cmr} establish a scaling law for critical mixture ratios in continual pre-training.\n\\citet{gu2025datamixing} show that data mixing can induce phase transitions in knowledge acquisition, a direct theoretical analogue to our detection boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Critical transitions and lead-lag methodology.}\n\\citet{scheffer2009early} established that systems approaching tipping points exhibit generic early-warning signals (rising autocorrelation and variance); \\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a practical toolkit for estimating such signals, while \\citet{boettiger2012limits} quantified detection limits in short time series---directly relevant to iterative training where $T{=}5$--$20$.\n\\citet{truong2026phase} prove that entropy collapse in iterative training is a first-order (discontinuous) phase transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down (CSD) early-warning signals.\nOur Granger-precedence approach circumvents this limitation by detecting temporal predictive improvement rather than variance/autocorrelation signatures, achieving ${\\geq}5/8$ forward detection in 12 of 15 above-boundary cells (three sync-dip exceptions at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ dissolve by $T{=}25$; \\S\\ref{sec:t20}).\nThe standard framework for testing temporal precedence is Granger causality~\\cite{granger1969investigating}, with neural extensions for nonlinear settings~\\citep{tank2021neural,marcinkevics2022neural,zeng2025kangci}; however, non-stationary series inflate rejection rates~\\cite{granger1974spurious}, and the Toda--Yamamoto procedure~\\cite{toda1995statistical} addresses this at reduced degrees of freedom severe in short series.\nWe use classical rather than neural Granger methods because $T{\\leq}25$ provides insufficient observations for neural network parameter estimation.\nThese methods are largely absent from the model collapse literature.\nOur analysis studies iterative from-scratch retraining; online or continual learning settings, where the model updates incrementally on streaming data, may exhibit different detection dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Analytical robustness.}\n\\citet{bell2022modeling} introduced multiverse analysis to ML, \\citet{coretta2023multidimensional} demonstrated that 46 teams reached different conclusions on identical data, and \\citet{kapoor2024reforms} synthesized these concerns into a consensus checklist.\nOur work applies this perspective to model collapse: five methods across multiple contamination regimes produce conclusions ranging from universal significance to complete null.\n",

This pattern corresponds to 24/24 forward and 0/24 reverse FDR-significant canary$\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed, mauve excluded).}

48	\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}
49	\label{sec:dose_response}
50	
51	\begin{figure}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
54	\caption{PermG dose-response profile (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). Sigmoid fit (shaded 95\% CI) yields $\alpha_c{=}0.483$. Four regimes: blind ($\alpha{\leq}0.45$, 0/8), onset ($\alpha{\approx}0.50$, 6/8), sync-dip ($\alpha{=}0.60$, 0/8), and detection ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$).}
55	\label{fig:dose_response}
56	\end{figure}
57	
58	The PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; full per-method table in Appendix~\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals four detection regimes: \emph{blind} ($\alpha \leq 0.45$, 0/8 FDR-significant pairs), \emph{onset} ($\alpha \approx 0.50$, 6/8), \emph{non-monotonic dip} ($\alpha \approx 0.60$, 0/8), and \emph{detection} ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$, 6/8 across 4~seeds at $\alpha{=}1.0$).
59	At $\alpha{=}0.55$, PermG returns 0/8 FDR-significant pairs despite marginal $p$-values ($p_{\mathrm{fdr}}{=}0.062$--$0.092$); differenced cross-correlation detects 8/8 bidirectionally at this level, confirming that a lead-lag signal exists but falls below PermG's directional detection threshold.
60	At $\alpha{=}0.60$, PermG returns $0/8$ at $T{\leq}20$ but recovers to $5/8$ at $T{=}25$, confirming a transient dip that $\sqrt{T}$ power eventually overcomes (Appendix~\ref{app:t20_comparison}).
61	
62	Method choice is itself a major source of variability: at $\alpha{=}1.0$, direction-checked forward significance spans 0\% to 75\% (Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison}), with $\kappa{=}0.185$; a specification curve (18~variants; Appendix~\ref{app:spec_curve}) confirms onset invariance.
63	Task degradation corroborates: perplexity jumps $+31\%$ between $\alpha{=}0.50$ and $0.52$ (Appendix~\ref{app:task_degradation}).
64	
65	
66	\begin{table}[t]
67	\centering
68	\small
69	\caption{Forward-direction FDR significance rates (\%) across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, fp32; $3\sigma$ threshold calibration, lag-direction filtering for cross-correlation methods). Each cell: fraction of 8 validated pairs at BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$). PermG achieves the lowest null-control FPR among directional methods (seed-42: 0/8; pooled 6-seed: 11.1\%) with consistent detection at $\alpha \geq 0.50$.}
70	\label{tab:method_comparison}

88	\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}
89	\label{sec:t20}
90	
91	The detection boundary $\alpha_c(T)$ decreases monotonically: $\alpha_c(5){\geq}1.0$, $\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with $T^{-2.08}$ power scaling (\S\ref{sec:theory}).
92	The $\alpha{=}0.45$ column traces the sharpest validation: $0/8$ at $T{\leq}10$, $6/8$ at $T{=}15$ (dual-seed), $5/8$ at $T{=}20$, $6/8$ at $T{=}25$.
93	The systematic 2/8 gap reflects MAUVE floor saturation ($\mu{=}0.029$, SNR $4.68$ vs diversity $6.40$--$8.20$; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
94	Cross-seed replication shows PermG within ${\pm}1$, though boundary cells exhibit sensitivity: $[0, 6]/8$ at ($T{=}10$, $\alpha{=}0.50$); $[0, 3]/8$ at ($T{=}20$, $\alpha{=}0.60$).
95	Minimum $T$ for 80\% power at $\alpha{=}0.50$ is approximately $T{=}30$ (extrapolated from simulation; Appendix~\ref{app:power_sim}).
96	The $\alpha{=}0.60$ dip dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), validating $\sqrt{T}$ power scaling; predict-then-verify (commit \texttt{bad72cc}) yields 2/4 boundary predictions within CI (Appendix~\ref{app:t20_prediction_timeline}).

      "content": "\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\textwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead across all four seeds (shaded: inter-seed range). This pattern corresponds to 24/24 forward and 0/24 reverse FDR-significant canary$\\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed, mauve excluded).}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{PermG dose-response profile (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). Sigmoid fit (shaded 95\\% CI) yields $\\alpha_c{=}0.483$. Four regimes: blind ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.45$, 0/8), onset ($\\alpha{\\approx}0.50$, 6/8), sync-dip ($\\alpha{=}0.60$, 0/8), and detection ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$).}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}; full per-method table in Appendix~\\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals four detection regimes: \\emph{blind} ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, 0/8 FDR-significant pairs), \\emph{onset} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.50$, 6/8), \\emph{non-monotonic dip} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.60$, 0/8), and \\emph{detection} ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, 6/8 across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.55$, PermG returns 0/8 FDR-significant pairs despite marginal $p$-values ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}}{=}0.062$--$0.092$); differenced cross-correlation detects 8/8 bidirectionally at this level, confirming that a lead-lag signal exists but falls below PermG's directional detection threshold.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.60$, PermG returns $0/8$ at $T{\\leq}20$ but recovers to $5/8$ at $T{=}25$, confirming a transient dip that $\\sqrt{T}$ power eventually overcomes (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_comparison}).\n\nMethod choice is itself a major source of variability: at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, direction-checked forward significance spans 0\\% to 75\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), with $\\kappa{=}0.185$; a specification curve (18~variants; Appendix~\\ref{app:spec_curve}) confirms onset invariance.\nTask degradation corroborates: perplexity jumps $+31\\%$ between $\\alpha{=}0.50$ and $0.52$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:task_degradation}).\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Forward-direction FDR significance rates (\\%) across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, fp32; $3\\sigma$ threshold calibration, lag-direction filtering for cross-correlation methods). Each cell: fraction of 8 validated pairs at BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$). PermG achieves the lowest null-control FPR among directional methods (seed-42: 0/8; pooled 6-seed: 11.1\\%) with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & $\\alpha{=}0.0$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ & $\\alpha{=}1.0$ & FPR$_\\text{null}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr          & 75   & 62.5 & 62.5 & 0    & 0    & 75  \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr         & 0    & 25   & 12.5 & 0    & 0    & 0   \\\\\nThreshold ($3\\sigma$) & 37.5 & 62.5 & 100  & 100  & 25   & 37.5 \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger       & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{0}   \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto$^\\S$  & 12.5 & 37.5 & 50   & 100  & 12.5 & 12.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\S$TY directional assignment under verification; row retained for completeness.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Pooled FPR (3 seeds, cluster-bootstrap 95\\% CI): \\S\\ref{sec:mmct}.} \\\\\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\nThe detection boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ decreases monotonically: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with $T^{-2.08}$ power scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column traces the sharpest validation: $0/8$ at $T{\\leq}10$, $6/8$ at $T{=}15$ (dual-seed), $5/8$ at $T{=}20$, $6/8$ at $T{=}25$.\nThe systematic 2/8 gap reflects MAUVE floor saturation ($\\mu{=}0.029$, SNR $4.68$ vs diversity $6.40$--$8.20$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nCross-seed replication shows PermG within ${\\pm}1$, though boundary cells exhibit sensitivity: $[0, 6]/8$ at ($T{=}10$, $\\alpha{=}0.50$); $[0, 3]/8$ at ($T{=}20$, $\\alpha{=}0.60$).\nMinimum $T$ for 80\\% power at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ is approximately $T{=}30$ (extrapolated from simulation; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.60$ dip dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), validating $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling; predict-then-verify (commit \\texttt{bad72cc}) yields 2/4 boundary predictions within CI (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_prediction_timeline}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}\n\\label{sec:generalization}",
